Concerns of global inflation returns as crude oil prices spike again. Asian markets ended mixed on Thursday.

The selloff intensified after a sweeping Democratic victory in the US midterm elections raised concerns over tighter AI industry regulations and a possible slowdown in data center expansion.

Traders also adopted a "wait and watch" approach to see how the Trump administration plans to resolve the U.S.-Iran standoff.

The Australian stock market is trading significantly lower on Friday, reversing some of the sharp gains in the previous session, following the broadly negative cues from Wall Street overnight. The benchmark S&P/ASX 200 is falling below the 9,000 mark, with weakness in mining and technology stocks partially offset by gains in financial stocks.

The benchmark S&P/ASX 200 Index is losing 87.80 points or 0.97 percent to 8,977.30, after hitting a low of 8,923.30 earlier. The broader All Ordinaries Index is down 101.60 points or 1.10 percent to 9,103.60. Australian stocks closed sharply higher on Thursday.

Among major miners, Mineral Resources is sliding almost 5 percent, BHP Group is declining almost 4 percent, Fortescue is down more than 2 percent and Rio Tinto is losing more than 3 percent.

Oil stocks are mostly lower. Woodside Energy is edging up 0.2 percent, while Santos is declining almost 2 percent and Beach energy is losing more than 1 percent. Origin Energy is flat.

Among tech stocks, Afterpay and Square-owner Block is losing more than 2 percent, Zip is sliding almost 5 percent, Appen is tumbling almost 6 percent, Xero slipping almost 2 percent and WiseTech Global is down almost 1 percent.

Among the big four banks, National Australia Bank is advancing more than 3 percent and ANZ Banking is gaining more than 1 percent, while Commonwealth Bank and Westpac are adding almost 2 percent each.

Gold miners are weak. Northern Star Resources is losing more than 2 percent, Genesis Minerals is sliding more than 4 percent and Newmont is down almost 1 percent, while Evolution Mining and Resolute Mining are declining more than 3 percent each.

In the currency market, the Aussie dollar is trading at $0.692 on Friday.

The Japanese market is trading sharply lower on Friday, extending the losses in the previous three sessions, following the broadly negative cues from Wall Street overnight. The Nikkei 225 is plunging more than 5 percent to well below the 57,900 level, with a sell-off across most sectors led by index heavyweights, financial and technology stocks.

The benchmark Nikkei 225 Index closed the morning session at 57,858.61, down 3,219.29 points or 5.27 percent, after hitting a low of 56,745.81 earlier. Japanese shares ended sharply lower on Thursday.

Market heavyweight SoftBank Group is plunging more than 11 percent and Uniqlo operator Fast Retailing is losing almost 3 percent. Among automakers, Toyota is declining more than 3 percent and Honda is slipping almost 4 percent.

In the tech space, Advantest is plunging more than 11 percent, Screen Holdings is tumbling more than 5 percent and Tokyo Electron is sliding more than 8 percent.

In the banking sector, Sumitomo Mitsui Financial and Mitsubishi UFJ Financial are sliding more than 6 percent each, while Mizuho Financial is tumbling more than 8 percent.

Among the major exporters, Mitsubishi Electric is sliding almost 8 percent, Panasonic is tumbling almost 10 percent, Sony is edging down 0.1 percent and Canon is losing more than 2 percent.

Among other major losers, Ibiden and Furukawa Electric are plummeting almost 15 percent each, while Fujikura and Resonac Holdingsare plunging almosy 14 percent each. Kioxia Holdings and Murata Manufacturing are tumbling more than 13 percent each. Tokai Carbon and Mitsui Kinzoku are sliding almost 13 percent each, while JX Advanced Metals is slipping more than 12 percent, while Ajinomoto and Sumco are declining almost 12 percent each. Japan Steel Works is losing more than 11 percent.

Conversely, Daikin Industries is gaining almost 3 percent.

In the currency market, the U.S. dollar is trading in the higher 157 yen-range on Friday.

Elsewhere in Asia, Taiwan and South Korea are plunging 6.2 and 5.7 percent, respectively. Singapore is slipping 2.2 percent, while China, Malaysia and Indonesia are lower by between 1.1 and 1.6 percent each. New Zealand and Hong Kong are down 0.6 percent each.

On Wall Street, stocks showed a substantial move back to the downside during trading on Thursday following the rally seen in the previous session. The major averages all moved sharply lower, more than offsetting yesterday's gains.

The major averages ended the day just off their lows of the session. The Nasdaq nosedive 1,421.52 points or 5.5 percent to 24,315.00, the S&P 500 plummeted 334.89 points or 4.4 percent to 7,353.73 and the Dow plunged 1,833.06 or 3.4 percent to 51,523.06.

Meanwhile, the major European markets ended the day mixed. While the French CAC 40 Index rose by 0.3 percent, the German DAX Index and the U.K.'s FTSE 100 Index both fell by 0.4 percent.

Crude oil prices surged on Thursday as investors look for the next steps to resolve the U.S.-Iran standoff - either through diplomacy or hostility. West Texas Intermediate crude for December was up $2.29 or 2.60 percent at $90.41 per barrel.

### 11. [What Anthropic's roadshow video says about its IPO pitch](https://www.businessinsider.com/anthropic-ipo-roadshow-video-pitch-safety-compute-2026-11)
- **Outlet(s):** Business Insider | **Published:** `2026-11-10` | **Sentiment:** `POSITIVE` | **Positioning Relevance Score:** `0.84`
- **Syndicated URLs:** [link](https://ct.moreover.com/?a=60820486823&p=21l&v=1&x=OZA0JySxmoEyWS65m5zo3Q)
- **Matched Innodata Pillars:** Agent Reliability Engineering (ARE), Infrastructure Engineering
- **Matched Competitors:** Palantir, Turing
- **Coverage Snippet:** •Words matter in an IPO pitch.

•Business Insider analyzed Anthropic's 42-minute roadshow video to quantify its themes by word frequency.

•Compute is king, but safety is still the company's foundation.

It takes Dario Amodei just over 30 seconds to mention the risk of AI-created bioweapons in his IPO pitch.

The Anthropic CEO is the first face you see in the company's IPO roadshow video it released on Tuesday. He stays on message, calling AI "the most promising technology in human history" that is "capturing the essence of cognition." He emphasizes that safety is top of mind for the AI lab.

The video is Anthropic's values and IPO pitch bottled up together in a 42-minute cocktail it hopes will convince Wall Street to invest in its public offering. Every word counts - so we counted.

Business Insider asked ChatGPT and Claude to separately analyze a transcript of the video to identify the most prevalent words and themes. The video itself is a mixture of brightly colored graphics that have become a staple of Anthropic's research and a series of testimonials from top customers, including Bristol Myers Squibb and Accenture.

Compute is king, once you exclude the expected mentions of "Claude" and "models."

Rank Word/group Mentions
1 Claude 65
2 Model(s) 58
3 Compute/computing/computation 49
4 Customer(s) 42
5 Work/working/workers 39
6 AI 39
7 Frontier 32
8 Anthropic 32
9 Capability/capabilities/capable 27
10 Safe/safe/safer/safely/safeguards 27

OpenAI CEO Sam Altmanlikes to say compute is destiny. Compute is arguably the reason Anthropic is going public, as financing data centers and the chips that fill them is expensive. According to its amended S1, Anthropic is set to spend $895 billion on compute and infrastructure, nearly half of which is expected to be spent by the end of 2030.

Anthropic's safety mission is the cornerstone of the AI startup. The 27 mentions also don't factor in things like Amodei's mention of the risk of AI enabling the creation of bioweapons.

If you analyze by theme, safety becomes more prevalent (though it still doesn't trump compute).

Rank Theme Combined mentions
1 Models and training 83
2 Compute and infrastructure 81
3 Financial performance and growth 77
4 Efficiency and value 69
5 Customers and enterprise 62
6 Safety, alignment, trust, security, and risk 55
7 Research and discovery 42
8 Coding and software 41
9 Products and platforms 34

Anthropic is quick to explain that it doesn't view safety as an obstacle.

"We're finding safety doesn't hold us back," the roadshow video narrator explains. "In fact, it's because we can trust Claude's work that we can take its discoveries and apply them in the real world in ways that will benefit everyone."

UNITED STATES EDITION

07 November 2026 00:43 EST

NEW YORK, Nov. 05, 2026 — EXL Service Holdings, Inc. (NASDAQ: EXLS) completed the acquisition of Aimpoint Digital, a data and AI consulting firm known for building enterprise AI platforms on platforms including Anthropic, Databricks, and Snowflake. The deal combines Aimpoint Digital's forward-deployed AI engineering with EXL's industry expertise, agentic AI capabilities, and scale to accelerate enterprise AI transformation.

Financial terms were not disclosed. The combined company will extend EXL's ability to support clients across the AI lifecycle, from strategy and architecture to deployment and managed operations, reducing handoffs and delivering clearer accountability.

The integration adds approximately 250 experienced AI engineers, platform architects, analytics consultants, decision scientists, and AI strategists to EXL's capabilities and expands its partner ecosystem with Anthropic, Databricks, and Snowflake, enabling faster production on trusted platforms. This acquisition also complements EXL's recently announced integration with iMerit to support end-to-end AI strategy, architecture, engineering, deployment, and managed operations

UNITED STATES EDITION

06 November 2026 10:16 EST

NEW YORK, Nov. 05, 2026 — EXL Service Holdings, Inc. (NASDAQ: EXLS) has acquired Aimpoint Digital, a data and AI consulting and platform engineering firm known for building enterprise AI platforms with deep expertise in Anthropic, Databricks, and Snowflake. The combination unites Aimpoint Digital's forward-deployed AI engineering and platform capabilities with EXL's industry knowledge, agentic AI capabilities, and operational scale to transform mission-critical enterprise workflows.

Financial terms were not disclosed. The transaction strengthens EXL's ability to support clients across the full AI transformation lifecycle and expands its partner ecosystem. The merged entity will scale Aimpoint Digital's capabilities across more workflows and functions, broadening EXL's enterprise-wide transformation offerings and accelerating progress from AI strategy to production and measurable business outcomes.

Rohit Kapoor, chairman and chief executive officer of EXL, said the combined team will turn AI strategy into production reality on the platforms clients trust most, delivering a single, accountable partner from strategy and architecture through deployment and managed AI operations. Aimpoint Digital's chief executive and cofounder, Ben Yopp, noted that the collaboration enables deeper AI strategy and engineering work at a larger scale, leveraging EXL's global reach and operational capabilities. The union adds approximately 250 experienced AI engineers, platform architects, analytics consultants, decision scientists, and AI strategists to EXL's data and AI capabilities, expanding its partner footprint with Anthropic, Databricks, and Snowflake and speeding client production on these platforms. The deal also builds on EXL's recent acquisition of iMerit to extend its end-to-end AI strategy, architecture, engineering, deployment, and managed operations

### 22. [I landed 4 job offers out of grad school and picked Meta. These 2 tactics helped me land my ideal offer.](https://www.businessinsider.com/meta-superintelligence-labs-4-job-offers-hunt-advice-strategy-2026-11)
- **Outlet(s):** Business Insider | **Published:** `2026-11-11` | **Sentiment:** `POSITIVE` | **Positioning Relevance Score:** `0.72`
- **Syndicated URLs:** [link](https://ct.moreover.com/?a=60823060453&p=21l&v=1&x=tYtPNOW2YUTgsCRC2MJVhw)
- **Matched Innodata Pillars:** Data Engineering
- **Matched Competitors:** Palantir
- **Coverage Snippet:** •Adwait Sathe, a data privacy engineer at Meta, went into his job hunt five years ago with a plan.

•He was strategic about who he cold-messaged on LinkedIn, and how he tailored his CV to each application.

•He said that if he were applying for the same roles today, he would be sure to stack up on side projects.

This as-told-to essay is based on a conversation with Adwait Sathe, a 32-year-old senior data privacy engineer at Meta Superintelligence Labs. It has been edited for length and clarity.

I've been working at Meta for the last five years, first at Messenger, and for the last three years as a data privacy engineer at Meta Superintelligence Labs.

I moved to the US from India in 2019 to pursue a master's degree in information systems at Northeastern University in Boston, graduating in 2021.

I knew the job application process out of college would be competitive, so I started my job search about a year and a half before I graduated.

I was very clear from the start: I want to work in data engineering privacy. I took classes in that field and built a relevant portfolio through internships and side projects.

In my second semester, I started looking at job postings I would apply for a year later to understand what the industry was looking for.

I had four offers in hand when I graduated - from McKinsey, Walmart, Amazon, and Meta. Here's the strategy I used to approach my job search.

Cold messaging people who weren't too senior

I had a tactic for cold messaging people on LinkedIn.

I connected with a lot of people on LinkedIn who were working on things similar to what I wanted to work on.

The people I reached out to were active on LinkedIn and working in Big Tech in data engineering roles, not managerial roles. Engineers and senior engineers, not vice presidents or directors.

I was looking for mentorship. I approached them to understand the industry's requirements, so that when I applied, my application would stand out among the masses.

My cold message was very simple: "Hey, I am Adwait, I'm studying this, and I want to pursue my career in this. I have this tech stack and résumé; what should I improve?"

One connection gave me excellent advice - that my résumé made it look like I wasn't experienced.

I had three years of work experience in India after getting my bachelor's degree. Still, when I first put together my résumé, it looked like a new grad résumé because I front-loaded my education over my work experience.

I started getting more interviews when I fixed that issue.

When I started cold messaging with this strategy, I aimed for volume, sending messages to a lot of people. Many didn't reply.

But once I had gotten three or four good mentors, I eased up on messaging, knowing that these people were really solid and would help me out.

I've tried to pass it on. Now, I reply to almost all LinkedIn messages I receive that ask for career advice.

Splitting job apps by volume and strategy

My second job search strategy was to split my application process into two buckets.

The first bucket: companies I really wanted to work for.

I had a list of companies I wanted to work for, and I used to go to their career websites a couple of times a week to check for new positions and apply early. I would be looking out for new grad positions, which are typically released way before graduation season.

The second bucket was wider. This was all about volume, applying to as many roles as possible.

I had a generic résumé for all the volume-based applications. But for the ones I really wanted, I tailored each application.

After I got my first offer, a data engineering role at Walmart, I stopped applying in volume. The strategy after that was to apply for roles I really wanted, which could match or exceed what I'd already gotten.

How I would apply differently today with AI

If I were applying for the same roles today, I would make sure to have a lot of different projects in my portfolio, because building has become so important.

A working product, which is live and has real users, is way better than bringing a prototype into the interview and saying, "I built this prototype, it worked when I built it, but it's not working now."

The second thing is AI fluency. This doesn't just mean understanding different tools, but also how AI works in the background and a judgment of when to use which tools.

But the fundamentals don't change. Being good at coding and knowing how to structure the code is still important. AI can handle implementation, but designing systems and architecture is still very important and human.

### 28. [Bosses are rethinking whether you get access to certain AI tools](https://www.businessinsider.com/cisco-goldman-jpmorgan-reconsidering-ai-access-work-2026-11)
- **Outlet(s):** Business Insider | **Published:** `2026-11-11` | **Sentiment:** `NEUTRAL` | **Positioning Relevance Score:** `0.60`
- **Syndicated URLs:** [link](https://ct.moreover.com/?a=60824443541&p=21l&v=1&x=BuKLI8BlRWmAYMDIiiOObw)
- **Matched Competitors:** Palantir
- **Coverage Snippet:** •Employers, including Goldman Sachs, Cisco, and JPMorgan, are putting new guardrails on AI use.

•The aim isn't necessarily to cut spending, but to make sure they're getting the most out of the tech.

•The increased oversight comes after some firms highlighted workers' voracious AI token consumption.

Your favorite AI tool at work might soon come with a budget, a gatekeeper, or both.

After years of urging staff to embrace the technology, companies including Goldman Sachs, Cisco, and JPMorgan are taking a more targeted approach to deploying it. From setting budgets and tracking usage to restricting access to certain employees or tools, the different strategies come as companies pour ever-larger sums into AI and seek to reduce security risks.

Many employers are realizing that AI use is "really picking up" and they need to set guardrails, said Lari Hämäläinen, a senior partner in McKinsey & Company's technology, media, and telecommunications practice.

At Goldman Sachs, CEO and chairman David Solomon said this month that while the bank is rapidly expanding the range of AI tools it provides to its 45,000 employees, it doesn't make sense for all of them to have access to the most advanced models every day.

"We need frontier models for certain things, particularly cyber," he told Bloomberg TV. However, "there are a lot of things where we don't need the frontier" to help employees be more productive.

Other companies are limiting AI spending after first using looser policies to drive adoption. Cisco's chief product officer, Jeetu Patel, compared the company's early approach to learning to ride a bicycle: Coding teams got months of unlimited tokens to become fluent with AI before worrying about efficiency, he said last month on the CXOTalk podcast. As usage has grown, Patel said managers across his 32,000-person team would receive token budgets.

In September, JPMorgan imposed a monthly $2,000 cap on some engineers' use of Anthropic's Claude Code, Business Insider previously reported. A spokesperson said the bank was tying costs to productivity, quality, risk reduction, and business impact.

More tokens, more money

The move toward tighter oversight of AI in the workplace comes months after some employers boasted how many tokens their workforces were gobbling up.

Visa said it doubled its monthly token use from 1 trillion in February to nearly 2 trillion in March and that it was giving prizes to its AI power users. In March, Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang said the company was trying to spend as much as $2 billion annually on tokens for its engineers - and that he would be "deeply alarmed" if a $500,000 engineer used less than $250,000 worth.

Employers also rolled out dashboards to make workers' AI use more visible - and, in some cases, to encourage broader adoption. For example, Disney's dashboard showed its biggest AI users were making hundreds of requests per day on average and using tens of millions of tokens, Business Insider reported in April. Similarly, KPMG US said in May it hoped a dashboard for its advisory business would encourage more "frequent and sophisticated" use among the unit's roughly 10,000 workers. It has since discontinued monitoring AI usage through the dashboard now that staffers are using AI in their everyday work, a spokesperson told Business Insider.

Making every token count

Now, rising AI spending is prompting companies to shift their focus from expanding usage to maximizing their investment.

A new study from Menlo Ventures found that enterprise AI deployment spending reached $177 billion by September, up from $33 billion in all of 2025. Meanwhile, only 13% of about 2,000 tech executives polled by Gartner from May through July said their organizations had gained significant value from AI.

Companies' AI bills may be ballooning in part because workers have little incentive to curb their use, said Alex Sobol, cofounder of the Millennium Alliance, an invite-only community for C-suite executives. Without limits, "they're going to use it in every possible way they can," he said.

Broad AI access can also create security risks if used improperly - another reason companies might be getting more judicious. A 2025 KPMG survey of more than 1,000 US workers found that 44% were using AI at work without authorization or in inappropriate ways, while 46% had uploaded sensitive company information or intellectual property to public AI platforms.

Rippling, a workforce-management software startup, set monthly caps in August on AI tools after projecting token spending could reach 40% of its R&D head count budget. The company said those limits and more efficient model routing lowered that projection to between 10% and 15% while token use remained near its previous peak.

"The point isn't to cut AI costs," Matt MacInnis, Rippling's chief product officer, said in a statement to Business Insider. "We're actually fine with cost growth, provided it's productive."

### 32. [A college student thought he’d struck gold training AI. Then he says he got burned.](https://ct.moreover.com/?a=60824445152&p=1pe&v=1&x=KL4Gv0sZ9DhMnCXWi6InvA)
- **Outlet(s):** DNyuz, Business Insider | **Published:** `2026-11-11` | **Sentiment:** `NEUTRAL` | **Positioning Relevance Score:** `0.60`
- **Syndicated URLs:** [link](https://ct.moreover.com/?a=60824443821&p=21l&v=1&x=z-AkfGJFjwhmdINBx0dCPg)
- **Matched Competitors:** Scale AI, Mercor
- **Coverage Snippet:** Joel Thomas was hooked the moment he saw the pay rate.

Handshake AI, the startup behind a career-services app that his university recommended, invited him to apply in August for AI training work paying $50 an hour or more. That would be enough, he figured, to cover his college expenses for a whole year.

Now, Thomas says, he is owed about $11,000 in back pay for hundreds of hours of work he did on Handshake's platform. Handshake has denied his appeals. The company told him there was no record of his work on one project during the third week of August, though a Handshake pay statement showed he had completed hundreds of tasks that week.

He isn't alone. Nearly two dozen current and former contractors told Business Insider that Handshake this year underpaid them by hundreds or thousands of dollars each. Disputes from those contractors over the past six months totaled $64,000, the contractors said.

Handshake said that more than 188,000 contractors have earned over $500 million for AI training projects on its platform. Startups like Handshake have recruited armies of students and young professionals around the world to train AI models for tech giants such as Meta, Google, OpenAI, and Anthropic. The booming AI-training industry has come under increasing scrutiny this year for the way it treats that sprawling contractor workforce.

Pay disputes at Handshake arose for several reasons, a Business Insider investigation found. In many cases, contractors' pay didn't match the dollar amounts they expected because of differences in the way Handshake and its clients tracked time and pay.

Former staffers said insufficient full-time staff and pressure to deliver training data at a breakneck speed also contributed to the payment problems. Project managers didn't have time to address contractors' questions - such as when some contractors found that instructions for a task were confusing or contradictory, former employees and contractors said. Once a contractor completed a task, it was subject to review; if a contractor didn't understand the instructions and the task didn't pass the review, the contractor wouldn't be paid for the work, former employees said.

On some marquee projects, one person could be responsible for managing up to 1,000 contractors, former staffers familiar with the operations said. Business Insider reviewedbank statements, pay stubs, and hundreds of email and Slack messages between contractors and the company.

Thomas, a soft-spoken junior who in his downtime likes to hike near his northern Virginia home, said he has tried to stay positive.

"It's hard to do because it's still a lot of money," he said.

'Competitive pay, clear expectations'

Business Insider first reported in March that dozens of contractors were complaining that Handshake had denied them payments, and that one contractor had won a $6,475 judgment in small-claims court. Handshake, in many cases, told contractors in automated messages that their accounts had been closed because they had violated platform rules. In October, after Business Insider sent Handshake detailed questions for this story, the company introduced changes to its platform that it said would improve pay transparency for contractors, whom Handshake calls "fellows."

Handshake said it has madecontractor pay clearer with a new payments dashboard, more explanations for adjustments, and an easier dispute process. Handshake said it is also rolling out a new live timer to track work on third-party platforms. The company told Business Insider that any contractor who believes a payment decision was wrong, or who has new information, can raise it again with the company's support team.

"Our Fellows are the heart of Handshake AI," the company wrote in an October blog post. "As part of being a Fellow you should always know what you earned, how it was calculated and when it will arrive. That's a basic expectation for anyone doing work with Handshake AI, and we're committed to meeting it for every Fellow."

Handshake declined to comment on Thomas' situation, saying it doesn't comment on individual cases.

"We take every dispute seriously," the company spokesperson said.

On podcasts and in LinkedIn posts, Handshake CEO Garrett Lord has repeatedly pitched transparent, reliable pay as one of the company's advantages over its rivals, which include Scale AI and Mercor. He has said complaints about late payments at other AI-training companies contributed to Handshake's decision to enter the business.

"Most AI gigs = shifting rates, zero transparency," Lord wrote in a LinkedIn post last year. "Handshake AI = competitive pay, clear expectations, and real dialogue. That's what experts deserve."

The AI pivot

Thomas was a sophomore at Liberty University in Lynchburg, Virginia, when the school directed him to Handshake's platform, which hosts a virtual career fair for the school. He signed up for an account on Handshake in January.

On its website, Liberty University says it doesn't endorse content, jobs, or employers on Handshake.

"Liberty University cannot guarantee that use of any of these products alone will guarantee the user a positive outcome," it says. The school didn't respond to requests for comment.

Not long after he signed up, Thomas began receiving invitations from Handshake to work on AI training projects,which the company pivoted into last year.

Handshake, founded in 2014 by three Michigan Tech students, had started out as a career platform. Students who linked their Handshake accounts to their school could search and apply for internships and entry-level jobs, register for career fairs, and connect with employers and alumni, among other services.

Colleges and universities paid Handshake a fee to connect their students with prospective employers. As the AI boom became a magnet for investor cash, Handshake saw a bigger opportunity.

In January 2025, Handshake expanded into AI training, tapping its network of students and young alumni to help frontier labs train and evaluate their AI models. Handshake also rebranded, adding AI to its name.

A slew of startups like Handshake pay gig workers to help make large language AI models like ChatGPT smarter. These contractors do tasks like writing expert answers to sample prompts, designing edge-case questions to test models and expose weaknesses, and rating AI-generated responses.

Handshake's AI business has exploded: Lord said in August that its AI unit had grown to more than $1 billion in gross annualized revenue. The majority of Handshake's revenue, former staffers told Business Insider, now comes from AI-training contracts with five clients: Meta, Google, OpenAI, Nvidia, and Anthropic.

Racking up the hours

"Great news!" the email said. "We have a new project through Handshake AI that we think you'd be interested in."

Thomas had taken on a couple of small Handshake AI training projects in the spring, but stopped because the hourly rates were low - around $17 per hour or less. This invitation and another that arrived in August were more enticing. These two projects would pay $50 per hour and $65 per task, respectively.

He got right to work, planning to log as many hours as possible before his junior year started.

Thomasrecalls that he would wake around 8 a.m. or 9 a.m., have a coffee, and begin working at his laptop, sitting at the white desk in his bedroom. Wearing AirPods, he cycled through R&B from Chris Brown and Bryson Tiller. Back at his desk after gym and dinner, he worked until midnight or 1 a.m.

Inside Handshake, staffers were pulling similarly long hours, former employees said. The company was scrambling to deliver an ever-growing amount of training data to its clients.

Project managers used AI coding tools like Claude or Lovable to generate instructions and prompts for contractors, but they were sometimes contradictory, leading contractors to seek clarification on their tasks, former employees said.

Questions from contractors piled up on Slack, and project managers didn't have time to answer them all, the former employees said.

One of Thomas' projects was called Project Folio, a study of ChatGPT usage patterns for which he was asked to upload his past chat logs with ChatGPT. For another project, he had to evaluate ChatGPT responses.

As he racked up hours, Thomas could see his earnings ticking up on his Handshake dashboard. At one point, the number topped $19,000, he says.

There was something important about that dashboard that Thomas says hedidn't know.

Like most AI-training platforms, Handshake pays contractors in units that sound intuitive - dollars per hour, or dollars per task.

The dollar figures that Thomas and other Handshake contractors saw on their dashboards were estimates based on the time they had manually logged by starting and stopping a timer. The AI company paying for the project had another timer logging their work - a timer that the contractors couldn't see, former Handshake employees told Business Insider.

Before paying contractors, Handshake compared the contractor's self-reported hours with the client's timer.

Handshake didn't tell contractors what counted as billable time, or how it was calculated by the client, former contractors and employees said.

"Final pay reflects the work our systems verify," the Handshake spokesperson told Business Insider. "Where logged time doesn't match the work, we review it, and where we find inflated time we don't pay for it."

Have a tip? Contact this reporter via email at or Signal at shuby.85. Use a personal email address, a nonwork device, and nonwork WiFi; here's our guide to sharing information securely.

Handshake said the company is committed to paying contractors accurately for the time they work and making sure it's clear how much they will be paid.

When a contractor disagrees with a payment decision, "a dedicated team independent of the project reviews it in full and can restore all or part of the amount," the Handshake spokesperson said.

'This is ridiculous'

Thomas transferred to the University of Mary Washington in the fall. He was in the library during his first week of classes there when he saw a payment come through from Handshake. It was far lower than he expected.

Angry and confused, he went to Reddit, where AI trainers often talk about their work for Handshake and its competitors. He posted a screenshot of the payment and wrote: "This is such a scum thing to do and I am unsure what I should do. Just want to expose them as much as possible because this is ridiculous."

Another user suggested someone at the company whom Thomas could contact. Thomas says the response he received from Handshake wasn't reassuring.

By the time his next payment arrived, Thomas was braced for another disappointment. It, too, came up short, Thomas said. In all, he was paid $8,000 for what he believed was $19,000 worth of work.

He hadn't captured a screenshot when his dashboard showed $19,000, but he had captured one when it showed $15,741.23 "awaiting payout" on his account. He emailed the screenshot to Handshake support staff.

"I highly suggest you check your systems," Thomas wrote in an August 27 email.

Handshake told him that the "awaiting payout" figure was an estimate based on the times he had clocked in and out on the Handshake timer, and that before final payment, the company had checked his time against his completed tasks. For the period of August 17 to August 23, the company told him, "our project leads did not find any tasking that took place during this time frame."

This explanation seemed to conflict with a pay statement Thomas received for that period, showing that he completed 428 tasks for Project Novel and would receive no pay for them.

A Handshake spokesperson told Business Insider that contractors aren't paid for tasks if the tasks aren't approved by reviewers, and that the phrase "no tasking took place" is shorthand for "no verified work." The company may deny payment for time that exceeds a project cap, or for logged time that doesn't match the recorded work, the spokesperson added.

Handshake notified Thomas that it had deactivated his account because of irregular activity "that does not align with our platform policies and usage expectations."

Thomas remains cautiously optimistic about how AI might shape his career. He said he sees AI as a useful tool for assisting work, particularly in science, technology, engineering, and math. At the same time, he worries that AI will replace too many jobs and could make it harder for some people to find work.

He's still open to helping train AI models. But he's given up on getting any more pay from Handshake.

TELUS ( TSE:T - Get Free Report) (NYSE:TU) had its price target upped by equities researchers at Scotia from C$15.25 to C$16.25 in a research note issued on Monday, BayStreet reports. The firm presently has a "sector perform" rating on the stock. Scotia's price objective would suggest a potential upside of 27.95% from the stock's previous close.

T has been the subject of several other reports. ATB Cormark Capital Markets lowered their price target on shares of TELUS from C$14.00 to C$13.00 and set a "sector perform" rating for the company in a research note on Monday. Royal Bank Of Canada downgraded shares of TELUS from a "moderate buy" rating to a "hold" rating in a research note on Tuesday, August 4th. Canadian Imperial Bank of Commerce lowered their price target on shares of TELUS from C$15.00 to C$14.00 and set a "neutral" rating for the company in a report on Thursday, October 8th. Scotiabank dropped their price target on shares of TELUS from C$17.00 to C$15.25 and set a "sector perform" rating for the company in a research report on Tuesday, October 20th. Finally, Raymond James Financial set a C$18.50 price target on shares of TELUS and gave the stock a "market perform" rating in a report on Thursday, July 16th. One equities research analyst has rated the stock with a Strong Buy rating, one has issued a Buy rating, ten have given a Hold rating and two have assigned a Sell rating to the company's stock. According to data from MarketBeat.com, the stock currently has an average rating of "Hold" and an average target price of C$16.43.

ExlService Holdings, Inc. EXL remains focused on propelling enterprises to enhance their business models through data and AI, with an emphasis on sustainable practices. Wed Sep 9, 2026: EXL Launches ‘Go Beyond’ Brand, Emphasizing Data-Driven Transformation

EXLService Holdings, Inc. (NASDAQ:EXLS) has unveiled its new brand, ‘Go Beyond,' which encapsulates the company's commitment to innovation and transformation in the realms of data and artificial intelligence. This announcement comes as EXL celebrates its 20th year as a publicly traded entity, with the brand launch taking place on 10th September 2026 at a Nasdaq opening bell ceremony. The new branding aims to reflect EXL's unique position at the intersection of data, AI, industry context, and enterprise execution, driving measurable outcomes for its clients.

Rohit Kapoor, chairman and chief executive officer of EXL, emphasized that ‘Go Beyond' is not just a tagline but a daily standard for the company. He stated, "Our clients come to us to fundamentally reimagine how their enterprises operate. With AI transforming every industry, we've built something rare; a company with domain depth to know what questions to ask and data and AI capabilities to answer them at scale." This strategic shift aims to further solidify EXL's standing as a leader in leveraging data and AI for business transformation.

The rebranding initiative aligns with EXL's foundational purpose of finding better ways to serve its clients and symbolizes its ongoing growth and adaptability. Since its foray into data and analytics in 2006, EXL has consistently pushed the boundaries of traditional industry norms. This includes the recent acquisition of iMerit in August 2026, which has expanded its capabilities in advanced AI model training and frontier AI development. Today, over 60% of EXL's revenues are derived from data and AI-driven services, showcasing its market-leading growth trajectory.

Shirley Macbeth, chief marketing officer of EXL, remarked, "For 27 years, EXL has been built on a simple idea: We find a better way. ‘Go Beyond' is the brand that captures that. It's an invitation to our clients and employees to push the boundaries of what's possible and a reflection of the company EXL has become, a global data and AI leader that combines deep domain and context expertise with trusted execution to drive outcomes that matter." This statement underscores EXL's focus on fostering an innovative culture that prioritizes client success.

EXL continues to serve a diverse clientele that includes leading corporations across various sectors such as insurance, healthcare, banking, and capital markets. As the company embarks on this new branding journey, stakeholders will be keenly observing how ‘Go Beyond' translates into tangible outcomes and strategic advantages for clients in an increasingly competitive landscape. Thu Aug 20, 2026: ExlService Holdings Expands Credit Capacity: A New Era for AI Investments?

ExlService Holdings, Inc. has taken a significant step in its financial strategy by entering into a new US$1.00 billion senior secured credit agreement with a syndicate led by PNC Bank, as announced on August 18, 2026. This move replaces its previous Citibank facility and extends revolving and term loan maturities to August 18, 2031.

The new credit facility is designed to provide ExlService greater balance sheet flexibility, allowing the company to fund acquisitions, technology investments, and share repurchases. It features leverage-linked pricing and covenants that impose defined limits on debt and interest coverage, which enhances the company's ability to pursue its ambitious goals in the AI sector.

As ExlService Holdings pivots from traditional outsourcing to a focus on data and AI, the new US$1.00 billion credit line appears to support the ongoing narrative of AI-driven growth. This expanded credit capacity is expected to bolster ExlService's strategy to secure AI-led deal wins while managing the associated risks, particularly those arising from rising wage inflation and compliance costs, which could complicate pricing power.

The recent acquisition of iMerit, which has brought its CEO into ExlService's executive team, further underscores the importance of scaling AI data services. Together with the larger credit facility, this acquisition highlights the company's commitment to enhancing its capabilities in this high-demand sector, while also raising concerns related to execution risks in integrating new talent and maintaining resilient operating margins.

Analysts are optimistic about ExlService Holdings' growth potential, projecting revenues of approximately US$3.2 billion and earnings of around US$374.5 million by 2029. The new credit facility could reinforce this growth outlook; however, if cost pressures intensify more than anticipated, it may lead to a reassessment of the feasibility of meeting these targets.

SECTION 2 CORPORATE PROFILE

2.1 Activities

ExlService Holdings, Inc., a Nasdaq-listed company, is a global leader in data analytics, digital operations, and consulting services, primarily serving industries such as insurance, healthcare, banking, and retail. The company's core business revolves around leveraging advanced analytics, artificial intelligence, and machine learning to optimize operations, enhance decision-making, and drive efficiency for its clients. By transforming complex data into actionable insights, ExlService helps organizations streamline processes and mitigate risks. Among its fastest-growing segments are analytics-driven solutions and digital transformation services, fueled by increasing demand for AI-powered tools and cloud-based platforms. This focus has enabled the company to expand its client base and achieve strong revenue growth, positioning it as a key player in the evolving landscape of business process outsourcing and technology-enabled services. ExlService maintains a commitment to innovation and client-centric strategies, fostering sustainable growth in a competitive market. It is the NASDAQ's 6th largest Business services company by market capitalization.

This acquisition builds on EXL's recent purchase of iMerit, further extending its ability to support clients from strategy and architecture through engineering, deployment, and managed AI operations. EXL is a global data and AI company with over 74,000 employees spanning six continents

### 64. [Exclusive: Former Meta and OpenAI Contractor Sama Liquidates, Citing Lawsuits](https://wdctv.news/exclusive-former-meta-and-openai-contractor-sama-liquidates-citing-lawsuits/)
- **Outlet(s):** WDCTV.News, Head Topics US, DNyuz, AOL, Yahoo Finance, Time Digital, Light Space & Time Online Art Gallery | **Published:** `2026-11-10` | **Sentiment:** `NEUTRAL` | **Positioning Relevance Score:** `0.60`
- **Syndicated URLs:** [link](https://uk.headtopics.com/news/exclusive-former-meta-and-openai-contractor-sama-89697597), [link](https://ct.moreover.com/?a=60811026054&p=1pe&v=1&x=Y7F63AZ--OukQEKmq5GwoQ), [link](https://ct.moreover.com/?a=60810874838&p=1pe&v=1&x=0Qn-MN8MyFmWyQuO8W6QLA), [link](https://ct.moreover.com/?a=60810857630&p=1pe&v=1&x=IGev4mCjdILHN35MLoogQg), [link](https://ct.moreover.com/?a=60810836643&p=1pe&v=1&x=KrC8P3oa3CfDufNlPTZ4IQ), [link](https://ct.moreover.com/?a=60811063413&p=21l&v=1&x=QXKIIDGJqzPx4SZOluOH-g)
- **Matched Competitors:** Appen, TELUS Digital, Sama, TaskUs, CloudFactory, iMerit
- **Coverage Snippet:** The embattled AI data annotation firm Sama is winding down its operations and liquidating its assets, according to a letter to creditors reviewed by TIME.

The letter, dated Nov. 6, blames "declining revenues due to increased competition that was compounded by recent class action litigation that would have required significant resources and years to defend successfully." The letter does not specify which class-action litigation contributed to Sama's financial difficulties. The company has faced separate such lawsuits in Kenya and the U.S.

An unaudited balance sheet attached to the letter shows Sama had approximately $1 million in assets and $17 million in liabilities as of October.

TIME previously reported that Sama performed content moderation for Meta and data labeling for OpenAI, work that employees said exposed them to traumatic content without adequate safeguards.

Read More: Inside Facebook's African Sweatshop

Sama and Meta are facing lawsuits in Kenya related to allegations that former Facebook content moderators were exploited and illegally terminated after they raised concerns, and that Sama illegally suppressed a unionization effort.

Specifically, Daniel Motaung, a former Sama moderator who was fired in 2019 after trying to organize a strike, sued Sama and Meta in 2022, claiming violations of constitutional rights including freedom of association, dignity and fair working conditions, as well as alleged forced labour and human trafficking. His petition has since been consolidated with a separate one brought by former moderators who allege they were unlawfully made redundant and blacklisted. Sama has said it complied with Kenyan labor law, and Meta denied responsibility for the working conditions.

Earlier this year, Sama separately came under fire for its work handling data from Meta's AI-powered smart glasses. Workers told Swedish newspapers Svenska Dagbladet and Göteborgs-Posten that they were required to review private content from the glasses' cameras, including people using the toilet, changing clothes, and having sex. Sama was initially named as a defendant in several proposed class-action privacy lawsuits against Meta and glasses manufacturer Luxottica, but plaintiffs voluntarily dropped their claims against Sama in August. (Meta denies the claims and says it will fight them, adding that while it may review data from smart glasses to improve its products, it takes steps to filter out private information. Luxottica did not respond to a request for comment.)

On October 23, Samasource Impact Sourcing, Inc., the U.S. company operating as Sama, transferred its assets to a newly formed company, Sama ABC, to oversee their sale and distribute the proceeds to creditors. Sama ABC is administered by "professionals who specialize in liquidating insolvent or otherwise financially distressed companies for the benefit of their creditors," according to the letter. Despite the similar names, the letter says Sama ABC is not corporately affiliated with the company.

"Sama leaves a highly regarded legacy as being one of the earliest champions of impact sourcing: a model centered on deliberately hiring individuals from marginalized communities in East Africa," the letter states. "Through this model, the company directly impacted over seventy thousand lives with life skills and technical training and employment in digital work at high living wages and benefits. Its impact extended throughout the communities it served due to the material wealth ripple effect created by its targeted employment model."

The letter notes that a bank has a priority claim to "substantially all" of the company's assets. The letter does not say what the liquidation means for the ongoing case in Kenya.

An email to Sama's press inbox bounced. Sama's CEO, Wendy Gonzalez, did not immediately respond to a request for comment. Sama ABC representatives did not respond to emails or phone calls.
